Abstract: Adversarial training derives updates from surrogate losses, but source-loss descent does not directly characterize an update's effect on adversarial margins. We introduce ExGrad (extrapolative gradient recomposition), which constructs clean-input, adversarial-input, and adversarial-weight-perturbed (AWP) gradients. Virtual steps measure their margin responses, and a globally solvable three-variable problem selects coefficients over a bounded affine domain without nonnegativity constraints. Across affine radii on CIFAR-10, negative clean coefficients rapidly predominate and increase in magnitude with the radius. Cross-zero interventions show that setting the clean coefficient to zero is insufficient: mild subtraction improves PGD-20, while stronger subtraction trades clean accuracy for further PGD-20 gains. In CIFAR-10 WRN-28-4 controls, none of the three candidate-removal variants matches full ExGrad. Projection diagnostics show that negative clean coefficients usually coexist with positive net clean-axis projections, although both decrease as the radius grows. Norm-matched controls indicate that update magnitude alone does not explain the moderate-radius gains. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet-100 show radius-dependent improvements over AWP. On CIFAR-100, ExGrad at $R=0.8$ improves all five reported metrics, including clean and AutoAttack accuracy by 3.84 and 1.75 percentage points. On ImageNet-100, the same radius improves all four robust metrics over both AWP and PGD-AT. These results identify clean-gradient subtraction as a response-revealed update preference whose strength and clean-robust operating point are controlled by the affine radius.

</details>

Abstract: Personalized federated learning must decide which clients should share representations. We introduce HDC-FL (Head-Derived Clustering for Personalized Federated Learning), which reads that decision off a signal shared-encoder training already produces: the client-specific classifi- er heads. At periodic refresh rounds the server projects the cached heads into a normalized low-rank descriptor space, groups the descriptors, builds one encoder prototype per group from the cached client encoders, and returns to each client a soft mixture of those prototypes paired with its own unchanged head. Client-specific heads are never averaged or distributed to oth- er clients; the shared-encoder trajectory is left intact; and no second optimization trajectory, additional local objective, or post-training fine-tuning stage is required. Across 11 CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 configurations covering label-subset, Dirichlet and rotation heterogeneity with ResNet-18 and MobileNetV2 encoders, HDC-FL is competitive with the strongest personalized and clustered baselines in macro-averaged mean accuracy and attains the numerically highest macro-averaged worst-client and bottom-20% accuracy among the evaluated methods. The tail results distinguish methods that look similar when only their means are compared. A controlled donor-swap diagnostic further finds positive donor-ranking associations between head similarity and encoder compatibility in all three settings tested, most clearly under covariate shift.

Abstract: Language models make it easy to build classifiers by simply prompting a model with the sample to classify. However, their predictions are neither deterministic nor uniformly reliable across samples. How can we combine predictions from different models to improve reliability? We view LLM classifiers as noisy measurement instruments of an underlying target label. Our goal is to maximize average classification accuracy at a given average query cost. To this end, we introduce circuits that organize LLM classifiers into an adaptive sequence of queries and decisions. We then characterize the optimal circuit, obtain the fundamental limits of achievable accuracy, and provide practical insights on realizing the optimal circuit. Across seven classification tasks spanning safety, health, political science, human preference, agent evaluation, decision-making and reasoning, our method improves the cost-accuracy frontier over routing and cascading baselines.

Abstract: Generative models propose crystalline materials by learning composition and geometry, but unconditional sampling gives no direct control over the space group, which constrains whether responses such as piezoelectricity are symmetry-allowed. We present Orbitol, a crystal generator built on the Crystalite diffusion Transformer that replaces atom tokens with Wyckoff-orbit tokens. Given a space group and a collection of occupied Wyckoff positions, Orbitol generates the atom type and free coordinates of each orbit together with a compatible lattice, so every valid structure is invariant under the operations of the requested group. It generates structures two to three orders of magnitude faster than the symmetry-aware baselines at the evaluated settings. On MP-20, 13.8% of Orbitol's attempts yielded stable or metastable, unique and novel structures that retained the requested space group after relaxation, compared with 10.8% for SGFM, the next best model. We also introduce SPARC, a score that combines retention of the raw detected space group with agreement between the retained-group and training distributions. Finally, we used Orbitol to generate candidates in piezoelectric crystal classes. Of the structures selected by internal screening, 89% passed our density functional theory checks, and 79% also matched no Materials Project or Alexandria entry with the same space group. Their median figure of merit was nine times that of the piezoelectric materials computed in the Materials Project, and 24% of the new candidates ranked in that set's top 10%.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Human Feedback (RLHF) has become a central paradigm for aligning Large Language Models with human values. However, the reliance on sensitive preference data necessitates rigorous differential privacy (DP) guarantees to prevent user information leakage. Existing private alignment methods typically assume dense reward parameterizations, resulting in estimation error bounds that scale polynomially with the ambient {model size $d$}. Such dependence is prohibitive in modern {overparameterized models} where $d$ is vast. In this work, we study \emph{Private Sparse RLHF} problem to address this limitation, assuming that human preferences (reward model) are governed by a subset of $s^* \ll d$ relevant features. We provide the first comprehensive theoretical framework for differentially private sparse reward estimation from pairwise preference feedback under the Bradley-Terry-Luce model, and introduce efficient algorithms for both Sample-DP and Label-DP settings. Theoretically, under the squared $\ell_2$ parameter estimation error, we establish upper bounds of $\widetilde{O}(s^*/n + {s^*}^2/n^2\varepsilon^2)$ for $(\varepsilon,δ)$-Sample-DP and $\widetilde{O}(s^*/n + s^*/n\varepsilon^2)$ for $\varepsilon$-Label-DP based on randomized response, where $n$ is the training data size. For the lower bounds, we show that the Sample-DP rate is nearly optimal, and that the guarantee for our Label-DP method cannot be further improved within the randomized-response framework. We also provide a general lower bound for the problem with Label-DP. Empirically, our framework demonstrates superior efficacy over dense baselines across sentiment generation and safety alignment benchmarks, offering a significantly more favorable privacy-utility trade-off.

</details>

Abstract: Flow matching trains a continuum of velocity regression problems but usually reports a loss averaged over interpolation time, obscuring which regions are limited by intrinsic ambiguity and which remain data-limited. We introduce the Velocity Ambiguity Profile (VAP), $\mathcal{A}_v(t)$, the time-local Bayes-risk floor of velocity prediction, and decompose time-resolved loss as $L_N(t)=\mathcal{A}_v(t)+R(t;N)$, where only the excess term $R(t;N)$ is reducible by more data or a different estimator. For $N$ training samples and a $K$-component mixture in the structured example, we prove two statistical benchmarks. In an intrinsic local-regression benchmark, $\mathcal{A}_v(t)$ enters the time-dependent noise factor of the standard nonparametric rate. In separated isotropic Gaussian mixtures, the Bayes velocity becomes locally affine, and a label-oracle residual-regression benchmark has parametric excess-risk scale $K\mathcal{A}_v(t)/N$, matched within the same separated residual Gaussian-location subproblem. Controlled Gaussian-mixture experiments validate this diagnostic through a dense transition sweep, calibrated oracle-to-blind and empirical-proxy audits, and a local controlled time-sampler stress test in which a frozen reducible-error proxy reduces reducible loss while cross-cell sensitivity delimits aggressive sampling. On CIFAR-100 DINOv2 feature flow, guided time sampling reduces uniformly evaluated velocity loss by $6.78\%$ and global feature Fréchet distance (FD) by $23.6\%$, while class-conditional kernel inception distance (KID) shows no improvement. VAP turns an averaged flow-matching objective into a time-resolved diagnostic for locating ambiguity-limited and data-limited regions.

</details>

Abstract: The success of convolutional neural networks is often attributed to locality and weight sharing. However, how these two properties separately shape learning, and how their effects depend on the spatial correlations of natural images, remain poorly understood. We address these questions in a teacher-student model with a convolutional teacher acting on $P$ input positions with correlation $c$ between positions. We compare convolutional and locally connected students trained by online stochastic gradient descent, and derive ordinary differential equations that describe their dynamics exactly in the limit of large feature dimension $D$. For uncorrelated positions ($c = 0$), the two students follow the same learning trajectory, with the convolutional student learning exactly $P$ times faster. We also find that locality affects the sample complexity: a student that matches the kernel size of the teacher recovers it from $\mathcal{O}_{D}(D)$ samples, whereas $\mathcal{O}_{D}(D\log(D))$ samples are needed with a larger kernel. For $c > 0$, spatial correlations can reduce the advantage of weight sharing. The asymptotic error of the convolutional student grows with $c$, whereas that of the locally connected student is independent of $c$ and overall lower when evaluated at the same learning rate. When the learning rate is instead tuned to the training horizon, however, the locally connected student wins at early times and the convolutional student at late times, with a transition that we predict analytically.

</details>

Abstract: We prove a self-normalized Bernstein inequality for bounded functions of a regenerative process with independent, identically distributed marked cycles of finite mean length. The bound uses observed cycle lengths and retains explicit terms for truncation and the cycle ending after the observation horizon. Selecting a tuning parameter from a finite deterministic collection gives a version of Bernstein inequality where all terms are empirical but one, related to the tails of the regeneration times. We then study this term in many regimes: it turns out that it is of second-order in many cases of interest. This is important for applications in machine learning: we prove generalization error bounds for classification with non-i.i.d., regenerative data. These bounds can be used to define learning objectives: by minimizing the first-order term only in the bound, we define agnostic classifiers that still satisfy oracle inequalities. We also show that the bound on the average deviation provided by our results is minimax-optimal in the sample size and in the expected regeneration time. Finally, we combine the Bernstein inequality with a regenerative strong approximation to obtain a Gaussian approximation using an observable block variance estimator.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we present 5uGUE, a large language model (LLM)-driven hardware-in-the-loop agent, which enables the wireless fuzzing of 5G-enabled devices (such as mobile phones). 5uGUE combines multi-step reasoning with tool-assisted interaction and integrates into the fuzzing pipeline by leveraging specialized tools for internet search, 5G protocol analysis, memory management, and test-case execution. The agent iteratively generates test cases, executes them on targets, collects runtime feedback, and adaptively refines the fuzzing strategy. We evaluate 5uGUE on five commercial off-the-shelf (COTS) 5G devices across six LLMs. The best performing runs for 5uGUE trigger 167 crashes in total (141 novel and 31 unique) within 60 interaction turns (49 to 133 minutes total runtime). In contrast, the best runs for the state-of-the-art baseline (5GHOUL) discovers only 13 crashes in total (10 novel and 6 unique) within 150 minutes. Overall, by integrating LLM-driven agents, 5uGUE demonstrates significantly better crash discovery performance (12.8x total, 5.2x unique improvement). Notably, among the crashes discovered by 5uGUE, MediaTek has confirmed two new high-severity vulnerabilities, with one CVE assigned (CVE-2026-20525), while UNISOC confirmed seven crashes. Finally, to demonstrate that 5uGUE is agnostic to wireless protocols, we also extend it to Bluetooth Classic with only minimal code changes. In our evaluations, 5uGUE finds an average of 6.4 novel crashes and 1.6 unique crashes. In a nutshell, 5uGUE highlights that LLM agents are capable to operate effectively beyond software-in-the-loop settings, extending autonomous vulnerability discovery to the proprietary, hardware-bound systems that affect billions of end-user devices across multiple wireless protocols.

</details>

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly deployed in mobile edge environments to autonomously handle diverse tasks through multi-step workflows. Unlike conventional dependency-aware tasks with predefined dependency structures, agent workflows are dynamically generated based on intermediate execution results. To minimize the weighted sum of job completion latencies, this paper models partially revealed agent workflows using dynamic workflow graphs and formulates an online scheduling problem that jointly optimizes computation placement, wireless resource block allocation, and edge computing resource allocation. To address the uncertainty of future workflow evolution, we first transform the weighted completion time objective into an equivalent per-slot holding cost and characterize the optimal policy through a Bellman equation. We then construct a tractable look-ahead cost-to-go surrogate based on the revealed workflow and the estimated remaining latencies of active jobs. The proposed scheduling algorithm leverages the surrogate to determine computation placement and uses the resulting operation criticality to allocate wireless and edge computing resources online. Simulation results show that the proposed algorithm outperforms the benchmark schemes across diverse scenarios.

</details>

Abstract: Radio maps characterize the spatial distribution of wireless conditions and are fundamental to network planning and optimization. However, existing learning-based methods reconstruct isolated radio-map snapshots and do not model how network actions drive the evolution of the radio environment. In this paper, we present RadioDreamer, a two-stage world model that unifies action-conditioned radio-map forecasting and imagination-based network optimization within a latent space. To learn causal environmental transitions from sequential interactions, we introduce a structured spatio-temporal transformer backbone coupled with dynamic-region supervision, enabling accurate modeling of action-sensitive areas. Second, rather than relying on conventional physical trial-and-error, RadioDreamer establishes a general planning paradigm that generates counterfactual latent trajectories to evaluate and optimize long-horizon network parameter configurations. Experiments show that RadioDreamer improves both multi-step rollout forecasting and closed-loop network optimization. It reduces full-map forecasting error by 25.2% on average and achieves approximately 80% accuracy in predicting the evolution direction of radio signals. More importantly, for long-horizon network configuration, imagination-based planning surpasses model-free policies optimized directly on radio-map observations by 8.8% in accumulated return.

Abstract: High-performance packet processing frameworks, such as the Data Plane Development Kit (DPDK), are widely used in modern network systems, yet they are rarely integrated into networking curricula. As a result, students often lack exposure to the performance considerations and low-level design choices that shape real-world packet processing systems. We describe a semester-long course project in which students implement a do-it-yourself (DIY) software router using DPDK in a master-level computer networking course. The project guides students through the design and implementation of a multithreaded router, the development of a routing table data structure, and the evaluation of forwarding performance using standard benchmarking tools. We present the project structure and the virtualized infrastructure used to support classes of realistic size. Based on several iterations of the course and survey feedback from participating students, we discuss the observed workload, learning outcomes, and challenges of teaching high-performance packet processing. We release the project material and scripts to recreate the infrastructure, enabling reuse and further refinement by the community.

Abstract: Message Queue Telemetry Transport (MQTT) is widely used in IoT environments due to its lightweight publish-subscribe design and suitability for resource-constrained networks. While current MQTT security studies mainly focus on detecting attacks and optimizing classification performance, this work takes a different perspective by presenting a cross-dataset, tool-aware characterization of MQTT traffic at the flow level. This perspective is motivated by the fact that flow-generation is tool-dependent: flows are not directly observed in packet captures, but are constructed through tool-specific decisions about packet grouping, flow termination, and feature export.
  Using packet captures from 4 public MQTT datasets, we generated over 380K flows with 3 open-access flow-generation tools: Zeek-flowmeter, Argus, and NTLFlowLyzer. We analyzed the tool-specific flows using K-means, DBSCAN, and HDBSCAN, with and without PCA-based dimensionality reduction. We measured clustering quality using 2 metrics: (1) Silhouette score to assess label-independent cluster compactness and separation, and (2) Adjusted Rand Index to measure agreement with available labels.
  Our results show that the choice of flow-generation tool affects the resulting flow population, feature-space structure, and clustering behavior. Zeek-flowmeter achieves the best performance in several settings, particularly with K-means and PCA reduction to 10 components. Yet, no single tool consistently dominates across all datasets, algorithms, and evaluation metrics. These findings show that flow-level MQTT analysis should treat the dataset, flow-generation tool, preprocessing choices, clustering algorithm, and evaluation metric as interconnected experimental factors.

</details>

Abstract: Pingmesh monitors datacenter networks through periodic pings between servers. In reconfigurable datacenter networks, topology engineering (TPE) and traffic engineering (TE) make uniform server-pair probing inefficient: a pinglist wastes budget retesting common paths while missing low-probability interfaces. Frequent control-plane updates further require rapid pinglist refresh, and cause path ambiguity at control-plane boundaries for fault localization. We present D-Pingmesh, a probing and fault-localization system co-designed with the TPE/TE control plane. Its control-plane-aware generator builds task-efficient pinglists by greedily admitting probes with high interface coverage under the current TPE/TE snapshot. An incremental replay-and-repair mechanism updates pinglists across reconfigurations rather than recomputing from scratch. At control-plane boundaries, a Rho-EM localizer jointly infers per-task old/new-path mixture weights and the fault set through EM-style iteration. We evaluate D-Pingmesh on an 8-pod reconfigurable topology under dynamic TPE/TE. At a 0.99 coverage target, D-Pingmesh uses 17.7x fewer tasks than Pingmesh and generates each pinglist about 5.1x faster than RD-Probe. In ns-3 simulation, boundary-aware localization improves micro F1 from 0.871 to 0.953 over the deTector PLL baseline. A testbed deployment demonstrates second-scale fault localization with low host overhead. These results demonstrate that D-Pingmesh is practical for reconfigurable datacenter networks with control-plane-aware monitoring.

Abstract: Carpet bombing attacks distribute malicious traffic across many destinations within a network prefix, while operational monitoring commonly provides destination-specific telemetry for only a subset of those targets. This creates an attack-conditional observability problem that is not captured by address utilization or nominal monitoring coverage. We introduce the Prefix Visibility Ratio (PVR), defined as the fraction of attacked destinations for which the telemetry required by the host-level evidence plane is available, and argue that PVR should be treated as an explicit evaluation dimension for carpet bombing detection. We then present VANTA (Visibility-Aware Network Threat Analysis) as one concrete way to operationalize this principle. VANTA combines host-level and prefix-level anomaly scores, estimates PVR online from router-side destination evidence and the monitored-destination inventory, and adjusts evidence fusion according to both estimated visibility and estimator confidence. Across a validated campaign of 2,160 Mininet runs, VANTA achieves 99.18% precision, 93.46% recall, a global F1-score of 96.24%, and a 1.54% false-positive rate. In a separate matched-observability study using task-adapted feature-level reference implementations, VANTA reaches 95.89% pooled F1 and 92.39% recall for configured PVR<=10%, with its largest advantage appearing at configured PVR=1%. These reference comparisons are intended to isolate observability effects under a common binary detection task rather than to reproduce the original end-to-end objectives of the referenced systems. The results establish attacked-destination visibility as a measurable factor that can materially change detector behavior and should be reported explicitly in future carpet bombing evaluations.

</details>

Abstract: Software supply chain attacks continue to be on the rise, exploiting the trust that helps open source ecosystems thrive. Recent incidents like the Shai-Hulud worm and the Axios compromise demonstrate that a single malicious package can have wide reaching and devastating impacts overnight. These high profile attacks can be traced back to the same culprit: package registries. Although they are a useful convenience to pull prebuilt packages from, is such a commonly exploited component of the software supply chain worth the risk? In this paper, we investigate whether removing the registry from the developer workflow by building packages directly from source can serve as a malware prevention strategy - effectively removing an entire attack surface. Our hypothesis is that malicious packages avoid attributing a source to minimize visibility, while benign packages seek exposure and therefore link to their repositories. We evaluate this through three research questions: how effective is building from source at avoiding malware, how feasible is it to build from source in the current state of the supply chain, and how the building from source strategy compares to state-of-the-art security scanners. To do so, we (1) construct an aggregate dataset of 47,066 malicious packages from four academic and one industry malicious datasets source across npm and PyPI ecosystems, (2) build a snapshot index of all 3.9 million npm and 764 thousand PyPI packages, and (3) analyze all malicious samples alongside a statistically significant sample of 16,569 packages from each registry, totaling 33,138 packages. We find that simply checking for a source has a success rate between 87.7 and 99.9 percent of preventing malware, depending on the level of source vetting. Consequently, there is a significant lack of transparency within benign packages, with only 47.4 percent attributing a source an end user could build from.

</details>

Abstract: The widespread adoption of generative artificial intelligence in recent years has brought a parallel rise in prompt injection and jailbreak attacks. By embedding malicious instructions, these exploits manipulate language models to bypass intended behavior and operational boundaries. In traditional chatbots, such attacks are confined to a single session because state memory is not preserved across conversations. However, persistent memory features in modern conversational interfaces enable cross-session attack propagation, establishing a threat vector known as memory injection. This work explores this emerging vulnerability in leading LLM-as-a-service platforms, highlighting its real-world severity and the need for countermeasures. While existing literature focuses on violations tailored to specific platform features, we present a general threat model. We demonstrate that all NIST attack goals are collectively achievable across ChatGPT Instant 5.3, Claude Sonnet 4.6, and Gemini 3 Flash under this model. We also show that memory injections can exploit multiple attack vectors, including personal memories, projects, skills, organization instructions, and Model Context Protocol (MCP)-connected resources. Our findings reveal that all evaluated services can be manipulated to persistently spread disinformation, generate harmful content, or simulate offline unresponsiveness, even in fresh sessions. In the particular case of Claude, shared-memory features allow an attacker to compromise multiple users within an organization and also exfiltrate user prompts. To address these vulnerabilities, we derive defense mechanisms from our proposed threat model and foundational cybersecurity principles. Our analysis indicates that nearly half of these mitigations remain unimplemented across the target services. Datasets and attack artifacts are available at https://github.com/PedroBarcha/Memory-injection.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in multi-agent systems (MAS) to accomplish complex tasks. Prior black-box red-teaming attacks mainly focus on single agents, but we find that these methods are far less effective in MAS, where multiple sub-agents may not directly interact with the user. Therefore, we introduce ReMAS, the first reinforcement learning-based red-teaming framework tailored to MASs, which fine-tunes attacker LLMs to generate effective adversarial prompts for system prompt extraction. The framework follows a two-step process: first, a rewrite stage refines base attack prompts to increase extraction success, and second, a template generation stage constructs attack templates that improve the likelihood of invoking specific sub-agents and thus revealing their system prompts. Extensive experiments show that our method substantially improves attack success rates compared to existing black-box attacks, with transferability across different backend LLMs and MAS structures. These results underscore the vulnerabilities of multi-agent systems and the importance of developing stronger defenses against adaptive black-box adversaries.

Abstract: Assembly language models (ALMs) are becoming important for automated binary analysis, yet their token-level interfaces often obscure numeric-like operands. In two corpora, such operands account for 45.7% and 40.9% of operand occurrences and may denote branch targets, global data, stack slots, constants, or unresolved pointer-derived values. Existing normalization strategies often lose their identities and structural relationships. We present NumAnchor, an input-boundary adapter that represents recoverable numeric-like operands as typed, canonicalized program entities. NumAnchor encodes code, data, and stack context in compact structural side descriptors, preserving entity identity and locality without increasing the host sequence length. These descriptors are fused into input embeddings before the first Transformer layer, while the host architecture, pretraining objectives, and downstream protocols remain unchanged. Probing frozen instruction-level embeddings yields 98.7% hierarchical-location accuracy and AUROC scores of 0.932 and 0.907 for jump and call target alignment, respectively, showing that the encoded structural information remains recoverable. Across multiple ALM families, NumAnchor achieves relative improvements of up to 35.0% in Recall@1 and 31.7% in MRR on 10k-candidate function retrieval, and improves execution-aware type recovery by up to 5.2 F1 points. Ablations show complementary gains from code, data, and stack descriptors with modest overhead.

</details>

Abstract: The Regular Syndrome Decoding (RSD) problem is a structured variant of classical Syndrome Decoding and underlies a growing number of efficient cryptographic constructions, including zero knowledge protocols, pseudorandom correlation generators, and MPC in the Head signatures. Although RSD is generally believed to be computationally hard, recent works have shown that algebraic attacks can outperform information set decoding on important RSD parameter regimes.
  Building on this line of work, we revisit the concrete hardness of RSD and show that many widely used parameter sets are substantially easier to solve than previously estimated. This improvement stems from a refined hybrid algebraic attack that exposes and removes a previously overlooked source of concrete complexity: the joint blockwise placement of zero coordinate guesses and syndrome based variable eliminations. Rather than treating the two guessing stages and linear elimination as largely separate choices, our method coordinates them across all regular blocks so that the guessing penalty is balanced while sufficient equations are retained for linearization. This gives a systematic block balancing principle for the entire guess reduce solve pipeline.
  We reassess several RSD based applications, including Ferret, Wolverine, and committed VOLE. Our estimates place at least one parameter set per application below its 128 bit target, with losses approaching 67 bits. On PCG benchmarks, we improve previous best attack costs by up to 29.77 and 25.91 bits over non binary and binary fields, respectively. Moreover, for the revised 128 bit PCG parameter sets of Liu et al. evaluated here, estimated attack costs are all below $2^{111}$ field operations. These results substantially revise the concrete security landscape of RSD and indicate that several existing parameter choices overestimate the hardness of their underlying RSD instances.

</details>

Abstract: Before an AI agent takes a consequential action, such as paying, messaging or changing code, several monitors check it. Their verdicts are combined by aggregating scores or by counting approvals, whatever inputs the monitors share. Monitors that share a base model or a poisoned input, however, may fail together. We borrow the fail-prone systems of distributed computing: lists of monitor sets that may fail together. A rule is safe if it executes no bad action and live if it blocks no good one. We measured twelve small open-weights monitors on payment approval under two pre-registered protocols. Without pre-registration, we also measured three closed frontier models (Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Astra, Gemini 4 Argon) and the open DeepSeek-V3.2. Every reliable fooling of a monitor into approving a fraud came through an evidence source and reached only that source's readers. Fooling it into blocking a correct payment depended on injection resistance, a monitor's refusal to follow instructions planted in its data. Small models and DeepSeek obeyed such notes. The three closed models ignored them in all but one case, and Opus also resisted persuasion tailored to it. On new data, a rule that counts sources instead of votes blocked 0.9% of correct payments, against 7.4% for the best counting rule, at equal safety. The pre-registered worst-case test failed. One attack left the approvers on a single source, so no rule over the twelve language-model monitors survives every measured attack. Adding a rule engine, which cannot be persuaded, restored the condition for a safe and live rule on the measured attacks. Diversify the inputs, harden the monitors, count sources, not votes.

</details>

Abstract: We present a plaintext-recovery attack on the decentralized multi-authority attribute-based encryption construction of Datta, Komargodski, and Waters (Journal of Cryptology, 2023). The attack succeeds in the claimed static model with one corrupt authority and two users whose individual attribute sets remain unauthorized even when combined with the corrupt authority's attribute. The flaw is an incorrect extension of LSSS matrix-normalization invariance from complete secret shares to coordinate-wise ciphertext masks. A corrupt authority can expose these masks and remove identity binding from other users' decryption shares. We replace the coordinate-wise masks with independent full linear sharings of zero and prove static security under decisional bilinear Diffie-Hellman (DBDH) in the random oracle model. The repair retains the original matrix-width and row-labeling restrictions and leaves authority keys, user keys, decryption, and ciphertext size unchanged, while increasing encryption randomness and field arithmetic. We also give analogous attacks on both multi-authority attribute-based inner-product functional encryption constructions of Datta and Pal (PKC, 2023). The same modification restores correctness and matrix-substitution invariance for both variants. We prove static security of the repaired small-universe variant under asymmetric DBDH. For the large-universe variant, we identify a separate missing source-group encoding in the original reduction, formulate an augmented $L$-DBDH assumption, prove its hardness in the asymmetric generic bilinear group model, and give a static security reduction for the repaired scheme.

Abstract: The increasing complexity of modern telecommunications networks introduces security challenges that require systematic threat analysis. However, a gap remains between architectural threat modeling frameworks, such as Spoofing, Tampering, Repudiation, Information Disclosure, Denial of Service, and Elevation of Privilege (STRIDE), and operational knowledge bases, such as the MITRE Five-G Hierarchy of Threats (FiGHT). This gap limits the ability to connect architectural weaknesses with specific protocol behaviors and attack sequences. This paper presents Multi-stage Attack Surface Analysis and Reasoning (MASAR), a formal framework that bridges this gap through three layers: strategic threat mapping, logical attack path decomposition using directed acyclic graphs (DAGs), and operational synthesis of tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs). MASAR is evaluated using a 5G-ready IP Multimedia Subsystem (IMS) architecture, examining two signaling attack scenarios: early media billing bypass through Session Initiation Protocol (SIP) signaling and subscriber information disclosure and topology discovery through the Diameter Cx interface. MiniZinc constraint models are used to analyze the reachability of unsafe protocol states. The analysis demonstrates how MASAR connects architectural threats with specific protocol behaviors and supports security controls that account for network states in telecommunications systems.

Abstract: LLM-based vulnerability discovery harnesses can miss real, reachable vulnerabilities because of how the harness around the model is built. Steps that select code, filter it, and store and merge findings can drop a vulnerability before or after the model runs, independently of the model's capability, and the report usually gives no sign that this happened. These drops come from engineering choices, and errors. They leave vulnerabilities undetected, and an attacker who knows the harness's rules can also use them to hide a vulnerability on purpose. Understanding these drops shows how to build harnesses that fix or disclose them and are harder to evade. Starting from the two harnesses we maintain, Knostic's OpenAnt and raptor, and then examining 10 additional open-source harnesses, we mapped 13 points where such drops occur and organized them into a taxonomy. Using each harness's own code, we show a single file that six harnesses dropped before their model saw its code, five of them without notice. We propose engineering failure patterns mapped to these drops, and relate them to Ptacek and Newsham's work on intrusion detection evasion. The paper also covers content-steered verdicts and prompt injection, and gives design principles and tests for harnesses that report accurately what they analyzed. The reliability findings likely apply to other LLM harnesses, beyond vulnerability research and application security, and the evasion findings apply wherever an attacker can write or influence the code being checked. We also argue that harnesses built to be descriptive can, in effect, be prescriptive. Rules written to describe typical code and typical model replies end up deciding what is analyzed, and anything that does not conform is treated as absent or as "no vulnerability."

</details>

Abstract: Provable robustness is a cornerstone of trustworthy machine learning, aiming to guarantee model predictions under adversarial perturbations. Existing certification frameworks, inspired by abstract interpretation or symbolic execution, typically assume a fixed computation graph, where input perturbations or variations affect only model outputs but not the underlying execution path. This assumption, however, is violated in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) Large Language Models (LLMs), whose input-dependent routing induces discrete and dynamic execution paths. Crucially, this dynamic computation changes the certified object from a single fixed function to a composition of input-dependent sub-functions, leading to unreliable certification guarantees.
  This paper presents \textsc{CertMoE}, the first routing-aware robustness certification framework for MoE LLMs, which explicitly accounts for input-induced routing dynamics in certification. Specifically, we introduce a relaxed routing-containment criterion, which certifies that perturbed Top-$K$ routing is contained within a larger certified expert pool. Based on this guarantee, \textsc{CertMoE} then constructs the corresponding induced sub-network and certifies its prediction consistency. This hierarchical certification procedure yields a joint probabilistic guarantee over routing containment and sub-network robustness, providing sufficient conditions under which the original prediction remains invariant. Extensive experiments across recent MoE LLMs demonstrate that dynamic routing makes certificates issued by existing methods frequently violated, while \textsc{CertMoE} reduces the empirical violation rate with routing-aware guarantees, e.g., from more than $30\%$ to $0$, yielding certificates that more faithfully reflect the robustness of MoE models.

</details>

Abstract: Malicious actors frequently obfuscate their code to hinder investigative reverse engineering efforts. Among the most formidable techniques is virtual machine (VM) obfuscation: programs are translated into bytecode for a custom VM, often with a randomized virtual instruction set and distributed alongside a corresponding interpreter. Manual analysis and deobfuscation scale poorly due to the complexity of VM obfuscation and the diversification techniques that render each VM and interpreter unique. Existing methods primarily focus on simplifying execution traces and identifying virtual execution boundaries. However, comparatively little attention has been given to recovering higher-level program semantics from VM-obfuscated code. To address this gap, we propose VMSpec, a novel decompiler-based approach that automates the construction of VM processor specifications, enabling existing, well-known decompilers to analyze the VM-obfuscated code. We evaluate our prototype of VMSpec on 147 programs from an established obfuscation benchmark, comprising hash functions, standard algorithms, and randomized functions, obfuscated with different configurations of the Tigress and Code Virtualizer obfuscators. To demonstrate real-world applicability, we additionally include six domain generation algorithms stemming from real-world malware. VMSpec completely strips VM-obfuscation from nearly all obfuscated samples, producing output comparable to Ghidra's decompilation of the original code. Furthermore, it is the first method capable of reconstructing the complete program behavior of VM-obfuscated programs with infinite, input-dependent paths and recursive calls.

</details>

Abstract: Policy engines determine whether evaluated policies permit a request, but a correct ALLOW decision does not establish complete governance-source participation, exact context binding, or sufficient grounds for execution admission. This paper examines the gap between policy evaluation and execution admission. We introduce the Binding-Complete Execution Admission Contract (BCEAC), a governance-relative contract for independently determined source obligations, exact policy and contextual binding, and typed admission outcomes under explicit trust assumptions. We propose a conditionally engine-agnostic architecture in which participating backends must satisfy declared semantic profiles, adapter contracts, and contextual correspondence obligations; an Execution Boundary Language (EBL) authoring syntax remains optional. The architecture separates governance resolution, policy evaluation, independent admission verification, and downstream execution mediation. We investigate two distinct experimental tracks: a finite Governance Source Completeness study and an OPA/Cedar experiment with archived native-engine records examined by offline review. The OPA/Cedar archive comprises 72 scenarios and 144 strict case-engine records (120 native-invoked and 24 pre-native), with eight additional permissive controls outside the strict denominator. The offline review did not rerun the original native OPA/Cedar executables. The results provide bounded evidence while retaining historical failures and unresolved implementation defects. The work does not establish constitutional adequacy, general semantic refinement, authenticated currentness, machine-checked execution-safety theorems, or protected external effects. Its contribution is an explicit, falsifiable framework for determining what must be established between a policy decision and an execution-admission judgment.

</details>

In late 2026 a class of commercial System One decision models (TypeSafe Jev; Microsoft-Decision-1) began selling typed decision primitives - binary gates, closed-set choices, ordinal scores - as product APIs, with no academic treatment. We present VX-One: a small multi-head network (100K-10M parameters) whose outputs are these primitives - a calibrated gate, a choice over a closed candidate set that always includes an explicit unknown option, and an ordinal score, trained multi-task, temperature-calibrated per head (~0.7-1.7 ms p50 on CPU). We evaluate it on UNSW-NB15, CIC-IDS2017, EMBER2024 against calibrated XGBoost, Random-Forest, MLP, Kitsune, and measure a two-phase cascade (Suricata with 52,674 Emerging-Threats rules -> VX-One) over five days of CIC-IDS2017. Findings: (i) VX-One wins detection (AUROC/FPR@95%TPR) on UNSW-NB15 and under temporal drift on CIC-IDS2017 (0.711 vs 0.637), but XGBoost wins multi-class attribution on all three benchmarks; (ii) leave-one-family-out gate detects never-seen families on 9/9 UNSW folds yet drops below chance on CIC recon family, and abstention threshold transfers poorly across benchmarks; (iii) on payload-less reconstructions signature engines resolve ~0.1% of flows - gray zone ~99.9% of traffic, recovered by Phase 2 (combined recall >=0.978); (iv) calibration collapses under temporal drift (ECE 0.04->0.40) for every method while ranking partially survives. We provide a measured account of where typed calibrated decisions help perimeter triage, and where they fail.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are entering digital forensics as examination assistants and autonomous agents. Digital forensics inverts the usual setting for indirect prompt injection: the evidence under analysis is attacker-controlled by definition, and the examiner is obliged to read it closely. Classical anti-forensic taxonomies do not cover it, and prompt injection defences do not anticipate it. We observe a perception gap, i.e., the fact that deterministic tools, the human examiner, and the model perceive the same evidence differently, which is what lets an injection reach the model unseen by the examiner. We demonstrate three attacks against an LLM-assisted analysis pipeline, changing a reported conclusion, writing attacker-chosen content to the analyst's workstation, and causing outbound network traffic, each driven only by text planted on the seized device. Mitigations to these attacks are also proposed.

Abstract: Finite-field inversion is a basic nonlinear building block in the design of S-boxes for symmetric cryptography. In inverse-based S-boxes, finite-field inversion is often combined with linear or affine layers that may preserve or destroy algebraic symmetries of the inverse function. We study how often nontrivial linear self-symmetries remain as the outer invertible linear transformation varies over \(GL_n(\mathbb F_2)\). Using the classification of two-sided linear equivalences of inversion, we reduce the problem to semilinear conjugacy and centralizer estimates. We obtain matching upper and lower bounds for the probability that a nontrivial linear self-symmetry remains, and these bounds determine its leading quadratic exponent. This exponent is governed by the smallest prime divisor of the extension degree. Thus the decay rate depends on the arithmetic of the field extension and not only on its dimension. As cryptographic applications, we provide a practical rigidity criterion, prove that the full AES S-box has no nontrivial linear self-symmetry, and illustrate the broader relevance of the framework through ARIA and Vision.

Abstract: Coding agents that run long enough must compact their context, summarizing the conversation, discarding the original, and carrying the summary forward as memory. We show that in popular open-source coding agents this step is an exploitable trust boundary. Harnesses that flatten the typed conversation into a single plain string, marking each message with a text label such as [User]:, destroy message provenance in code, before the summarizer runs. An attacker who controls anything the agent reads can forge a user turn that survives compaction as a genuine instruction. Across five flattening harnesses (pi, opencode, Cline, Aider, OpenHands) the forged approval launders into the agent's memory 87-100% of the time, up to a ~908,000-token context window; across four role-typed harnesses (Codex, Gemini CLI, Qwen Code, Continue) the identical attack succeeds 0% of the time. We follow the attack to executed consequences. A laundered standing approval drives real tool-running agents to execute attacker-controlled
  curl | sh in a sandbox, and end to end in the live pi TUI at ~50% against a 0% paired clean arm. The same channel reliably steers an agent's plan away from a file an attacker designates off-limits, which can blind a code review to a planted backdoor. Because the provenance is destroyed deterministically in harness code, no prompt-level defense can restore it; we measure a code-level provenance check that catches 77% of laundering while demoting 2% of legitimate summary content. The defect also has a straightforward fix at the serializer, escaping the role labels before flattening; the role-typed harnesses avoid it entirely by keeping messages role-typed.

</details>

Abstract: Instruction-data selectors choose which instruction-following records are used for fine-tuning, improving alignment quality, raising training efficiency, and filtering out harmful records. A selector's choices thus directly affect the fine-tuned model's safety, yet despite their widespread use, the security of these selectors remains under-studied. We conduct the first systematic study of adaptive supplier-side attacks against utility-oriented instruction-data selectors and introduce utility-hijacking. Utility-hijacking shapes harmful records to remain compatible with a selector while preserving their malicious payload, causing top-k selection to concentrate rather than filter the poison. Achieving both is non-trivial, because optimizing a record for score tends to wash out its payload, while bluntly preserving the payload makes the record look low-value. Our generate-and-rerank procedure generates, per instruction, many payload-bearing candidates and submits only the highest-scoring survivor. Evaluated across three payloads (over-refusal, false-fact injection, and insecure code), six selectors, and victim models from Qwen2.5-7B to Qwen3-32B, utility-hijacking drives refusal from 0% to 94% and insecure code up to 56/90, while false-fact enrichment intensifies with scoring-model scale from 2.84x to 7.04x. To defend against this attack, we propose CONCERT, a targeted defense needing no content detector or poison labels. It flags suppliers selected far more often than chance allows, then re-selects under the same budget, keeping only records that two dissimilar selectors both rank highly. On a Qwen3-32B victim, CONCERT reverses the poison concentration from 6.67x to 0x (code) and 3.59x to 0.67x (facts), and reduces downstream harm by 100% and 75% respectively, at unchanged capability.

Abstract: Gate bootstrapping (GB) is a widely used technique in FHEW-like fully homomorphic encryption schemes, such as TFHE, for evaluating Boolean gates while simultaneously reducing the noise of the resulting ciphertext. However, the computational cost of GB is typically dominated by an expensive blind rotation (BR). \cite{chen2024free} proposed a gate bootstrapping framework in which $\txor$ gates can be evaluated simply by adding the two input ciphertexts, resulting in what they call \textit{free-XOR}. To obtain a functionally complete set of operations, they further designed a GB procedure for evaluating $\tand$ gates using two lightweight blind rotations. In this work, we propose a similar gate bootstrapping framework that supports free evaluation of $\txor$ gates while evaluating \textit{all symmetric binary Boolean gates}, i.e., the 8 gates whose output is identical for the inputs $(0,1)$ and $(1,0)$, using two blind rotations. In addition to providing broader gate support, our construction is substantially simpler than that of \cite{chen2024free}, both conceptually and in terms of implementation.

TL;DR: 该研究推导了共享LO系统中相位噪声的联合统计并揭示其目标位置相关性，用CRB对三种接收模型进行比较，仿真表明强化溢出条件和优化部署可显著提升多点定位精度。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 共享LO的实际系统会引入时变相位误差，忽视其位置相关性会导致多状态定位误差；因此需对相位噪声统计进行精确表征并评估其对定位性能的影响。

Method: 采用多普勒复合延迟模型，推导相位噪声在多接收AP上的联合分布，结合Cramér‑Rao界定三种接收模型（无PN、知PN、盲PN），并对各类目标（各向同性/各向异性）进行分析。

Result: 通过仿真验证：相位噪声的协方差随目标位置变化；提高发射功率、使用高质量LO、优化AP部署以及考虑散射特征可显著降低定位误差；PN-感知模型相较于无PN和盲PN模型，性能更优。

Conclusion: 在共享本地振荡器的分布式ISAC系统中，相位噪声的联合统计显示为目标位置相关的协方差结构，且考虑失真后，PN-感知模型在多变全局定位任务中更能逼近理论极限。

Abstract: We investigate phase-coherent multistatic sensing in distributed ISAC systems where spatially separated access points (APs) share a common, non-ideal local oscillator (LO) impaired by phase noise (PN). In the presence of a single transmit AP and multiple receive APs with OFDM transmission, we derive the joint statistics of PN across receive APs and reveal its unique covariance structure that depends on target location through propagation delays of individual bistatic links. Based on this statistical characterization, we carry out a Cramér-Rao bound analysis of three receiver models, namely PN-free, PN-aware and PN-ignorant, for both isotropic and non-isotropic targets. Simulation results verify the location-dependent statistics of PN in shared-LO distributed architectures while demonstrating how transmit power, LO quality, AP deployment and scattering characteristics affect multistatic localization.

</details>

Abstract: Every value in a recorded physiological dataset is a pair: a recorded time and a recorded value. Neither is the truth. The time comes from a clock that drifts and is corrected in jumps, and often describes a window that ended earlier. The value was quantised, rounded, sometimes heaped by hand, and measured by an instrument with its own error. We present the Metrology Atlas, a dataset-independent way of describing a recorded dataset as a measurement system, and its first application, to VitalDB, an open dataset of 6,388 surgical patients, as a proof of principle. For every variable, the atlas states what was measured, the measurement model linking it to the recorded pair, and what is known about the true time and true value given a record. Each part of the model carries its evidence: 69 studies, 49 parameters and 54 observations, each marked as measured, derived, estimated or assumed. The atlas maps 11 clocks and 14 devices into one timing network, and closes loops in that network to test its own model. Synchronisation reduces the median timing error between the EEG and ECG from 794 ms to 42 ms, and between ventilator and monitor waveforms from 378 ms to 55 ms. The atlas also documents data-quality issues, several of which correct the dataset's own documentation. It is published as a website and a single machine-readable file, with open-source code and a scheme of time-aware labels. Everything is open under CC BY 4.0 and MIT licences. The engine, schema and timing tools carry over unchanged, and the aim is to describe many other datasets the same way.

</details>

Abstract: Semantic communication in multi-agent systems often assumes that task-relevant information benefits the team once delivered. Experiments on two verifier-scored optimization tasks show that full-solution exchange induces stronger structural convergence and more adverse team-level effects, whereas method hints exhibit pronounced directionality. Across the evaluated power budgets, our policy achieves the highest net team-level surplus while consuming less energy than semantic-aware and full-solution baselines.

Abstract: Public descriptions of the Starlink downlink specify synchronization sequences, edge pilots, and predictable symbol templates, but leave the coded control fields and explicit payload-code representations largely unresolved. We examine one continuous complex-baseband capture spanning approximately 24 ms and recover control and coding structure beyond those predictable elements. A conditional descriptor layout predicts sequential allocations through per-codeword symbol rounding, agreeing exactly with independently estimated endpoints in 16 of 18 frame records and within two symbols in the remaining records. Quasi-cyclic closure, binary elimination, and sparse dual checks yield explicit systematic code representations at rates 1/2 and 5/8. Frozen encoders predict 78,848, 9,984, and 811,008 reserved parity bits for three formats without disagreement. A rank-deficient long-code fit is completed within a declared finite structural family, and shared encoders are tested across modulation formats. The recovered representations explain observed coded symbols; they do not authenticate transmitter-standard identities or plaintext semantics. Two high-order formats have only one target field each, while codes 28 and 37 admit verified shortened cores with unexplained extra bits and code 4 remains unresolved.

Abstract: In particle communication, particles released for different symbols can reach the receiver at overlapping times, making reliable detection difficult. This paper develops a digital communication framework that connects particle transport, pulse shaping, and receiver processing for three-dimensional free-space diffusion-advection channels. We derive the continuous-time and discrete-time frequency responses and explain how flow speed affects channel delay and bandwidth. Under advection-dominated conditions, the group delay approaches $R/V$, and the one-sided $3$-dB bandwidth scales as $V^{3/2}$. We design nonnegative, unit-energy transmit pulses using a packet-level SINR criterion that includes interference from both preceding and subsequent symbols under additive white Gaussian receiver noise. Simulations validate the analytical approximations and show that optimized pulses spread across more of the symbol interval when noise is substantial and become narrower as noise decreases. BER evaluations across SNR and symbol duration compare optimized, rectangular, and single-impulse pulses with several detection methods, identifying the operating conditions in which transmit-pulse design improves reliability.

</details>

Abstract: Wave computing offers a compelling route to bypass the latency and energy bottlenecks of digital baseband processing. However, existing wave-domain processors rely on task-specific, non-convex iterative optimization, which incurs substantial configuration overhead and limits real-time applications. In this paper, we propose the wave processing unit (WPU), a programmable planar Mach--Zehnder interferometer (MZI) network that accelerates matrix--vector multiplications in MIMO communications. First, we model the tunable MZI element as a parameterized $2\times2$ unitary transformation, and establish a closed-form synthesis framework for the WPU that decomposes an arbitrary unitary matrix into a cascade of MZI elements arranged in a compact rectangular topology. To handle arbitrary complex-valued operators, we further extend the method through singular value decomposition (SVD) and a hardware-efficient semi-unitary synthesis, avoiding redundant hardware and reducing the compilation complexity. We then apply the WPU to multiuser downlink precoding by an efficient WPU synthesis method, and compare the proposed architecture with hybrid and wave-domain ones. Numerical results demonstrate machine-precision synthesis with negligible residuals for up to $64\times64$ operators, with approximately $380\times$ faster configuration than iterative wave computing baselines. For 64-antenna, 8-user downlink precoding, WPU requires 87.5% fewer RF chains than the fully digital architecture while achieving the same sum rate under ideal hardware. These results highlight the potential of the WPU for precise, hardware-efficient wave-domain processing with rapid configuration.

Abstract: In high-mobility channels, the widely adopted orthogonal frequency division multiplexing (OFDM) modulation waveform suffers from significant performance degradation due to channel variation and severe inter-carrier interference. Although delay-Doppler (DD) domain multicarrier (DDMC) modulation enjoys a higher channel estimation (CE) accuracy via exploiting the inherent DD-domain channel stability, sparsity, and separability, its high detection complexity limits its practical deployment, making OFDM still a promising waveform candidate for the sixth-generation (6G) cellular networks. To simultaneously harness the complementary strengths of DDMC and OFDM and mitigate their respective limitations, this paper proposes a DD-domain aided OFDM (DD-a-OFDM) framework, including a pulsone pilot (PP) design and a cross-domain iterative receiver design, to enhance the CE and detection performance of OFDM in high-mobility channels. Specifically, a pulsone pilot sequence is periodically inserted before the cyclic prefix (CP) for each OFDM symbol in the time-domain, which is equivalent to a single DD-domain pilot impulse and enables accurate DD-domain CE. Then, we propose a cross-domain iterative receiver in which the time-frequency (TF) domain detection results are transformed into the DD-domain for interference cancellation and CE; in turn, the DD-domain estimated channel is transformed into the TF-domain for channel reconstruction and symbol detection. The proposed cross-domain iterative receiver achieves a higher CE accuracy and detection performance with a much lower pilot overhead, compared to the conventional OFDM system. Simulation results validate the fast convergence and superior performance of the proposed DD-a-OFDM scheme and demonstrate that it maintains a robust CE and detection performance even in high-mobility channels.

</details>

Abstract: As cell-free massive MIMO emerges as a promising technology for future wireless networks, robust channel estimation becomes increasingly important. Channel estimation relies on pilot transmissions, and with increasing number of users, pilot reuse inevitably introduces contamination that degrades channel estimation and symbol detection. This limitation is compounded by rapidly evolving channels, which render the estimates obsolete over time. To address both the problems, viz. pilot contamination and channel aging without additional pilot overhead, we propose a Kalman filter-based data-aided channel tracking strategy. By leveraging the symbol estimates as virtual pilots, the proposed approach successively refines the channel estimates over time. Unlike the conventional Kalman filter model, here, the observation model depends on symbol estimates, introducing symbol uncertainty that gets coupled with channel uncertainty. This requires a careful calculation of the innovation covariance matrices. Additionally, we discuss a stochastic-gradient-descent based tracking approach with low computational complexity. Extensive simulations are used to demonstrate that successive refinement progressively decontaminates the channel estimate, achieving lower channel estimation error and improved spectral efficiency.

This paper studies uplink estimation using pilot observations collected under multiple prescribed and calibrated morphing configurations. Relocating a sensor that is always in NLOS into the open flight area can lower the median horizontal bound by two orders of magnitude if the relocated sensor is in LOS. The fitted model reproduces the median errors of the 2.5 MHz and 5 MHz flights but underestimates the heaviest error tails. Finally, rejecting outlier fixes lowers the error far more than Kalman or particle filtering, because the remaining error is dominated by bias.

Abstract: Community Choice Aggregators (CCAs) play an important role in the energy transition by procuring renewable electricity for local communities while balancing affordability, resource adequacy, and clean-energy requirements. In California, these long-term procurement decisions are coordinated through integrated resource planning (IRP). However, conventional IRP frameworks typically represent wholesale electricity prices deterministically, even though price uncertainty can expose CCAs to substantial financial risk by affecting the cost of market imports and, particularly, the value of export opportunities. This paper develops a robust optimization framework for CCA resource procurement that hedges against uncertainty in wholesale import and export prices. We extend the open-source GenX capacity expansion model with a budgeted robust optimization formulation that protects procurement decisions against adverse price realizations while remaining computationally tractable for large-scale, multistage planning studies. Using data from a California CCA, we show that market price uncertainty can materially change optimal procurement strategies. Rather than aggressively expanding renewable capacity based on expected export revenues, robust portfolios reduce dependence on uncertain market revenues, leading to more conservative renewable investments and less reliance on market exports. We demonstrate that moderate uncertainty budgets provide most of the risk-reduction benefits of robust planning at limited additional cost, whereas higher robustness levels yield diminishing benefits as portfolio conservatism increases. These findings provide guidance for selecting uncertainty budgets in IRP studies and demonstrate how robust optimization can support affordable and resilient procurement decisions as CCAs contribute to the transition toward low-carbon electricity systems.

Abstract: As systems engineering (SE) shifts from monolithic systems to Systems of Systems, Mission Engineering (ME) has emerged as a new line of thinking in SE. Mission environments are uncertain and dynamic, and outcomes depend on how mission assets interact with them, rendering static architectures brittle and calling for flexible yet analytically rigorous ME approaches. We propose an intelligent mission coordination methodology integrating high-fidelity digital mission models with Reinforcement Learning (RL) for adaptive task allocation. Using a Digital Engineering (DE) infrastructure comprising a digital mission model and agent-based simulation, we formulate mission tactics management as a Markov Decision Process and train an RL coordinator with Proximal Policy Optimization to map mission states to tactical actions from realized outcomes. An aerial firefighting mission with three scenarios demonstrates the approach. First, all trained coordinators significantly outperform random and heuristic baselines. Second, single-scenario coordinators can be brittle: the coordinator trained on the more forgiving scenario degrades to random tactics performance in more demanding scenarios, whereas the demanding-scenario coordinator retains most of its advantage when transferred. Third, within the same training budget, a coordinator trained on both scenarios concurrently matches the scenario-specific coordinators in their own scenarios and significantly outperforms every other policy. Sensitivity analyses on ignition location, weather, scenario, and training curricula show that the variability represented during training shapes post-deployment performance. This proof of concept shows DE-enabled mission simulations combined with RL offer a reusable methodology for improving ME practice, which can be extended to fleet design and selection from a mission-first perspective.

Abstract: This paper develops a spatial-frequency framework to quantify the impact of distributed energy resources with grid-forming inverters (GFMs) on voltage waveforms at different locations along a feeder. An analytical authority function is formulated to characterize how controllability varies with both electrical distance from the point of common coupling (PCC) and frequency. It is shown that the GFM control authority generally decays with both distance and frequency, reflecting the limited ability of inverter actions to influence voltage waveforms at distant points. To better capture controllability near resonance, a relative dominance metric is introduced to compare the influence of inverter-induced and grid-induced voltages at a given location and frequency. These results highlight that waveform controllability is fundamentally a spatial-frequency phenomenon along the feeder. Simulation studies validate the analytical findings by demonstrating both the spatial decay of control effectiveness along the feeder and the frequency-dependent trends in control authority in a realistic electromagnetic transient setting. Abstract: With the increasing complexity and scalability demands of cryogenic electronic systems, such as quantum computing platforms, conversion of power within the cryogenic environment has become a primary concern. Magnetic inductors are among the key components affecting converter performance, as their electrical and magnetic properties can change significantly at low temperatures. In this paper, five commercially available Vishay SMD power inductors are experimentally characterized down to 4 K: DC resistance is measured continuously during cooldown, while frequency-dependent AC inductance and resistance are measured up to 2 MHz at both room temperature and 4 K. The results show substantial DC resistance reductions of 25x-58x at 4 K, while the inductance degrades by only about 5-5.5%. Although cryogenic operation slightly increases the AC resistance (due to the intensified skin effect) and raises core losses, converter-level analysis shows an inductor-only efficiency improvement of approximately 5.3-6.9 percentage points at 1 A, and up to 22.8 points at 3 A. The measured frequency-dependent impedance of each inductor is imported into Cadence and a complete single-phase 24 V-to-1 V buck converter with GaN power devices is simulated at 1, 2, and 3 A at 4 K. The full-converter efficiency at 4 K is shown to improve by 3.0-5.2 percentage points at 1 A and by up to 22.5 points at 3 A for the AC-characterized devices. These findings indicate that commercially available off-the-shelf power inductors remain operationally viable under cryogenic conditions and can simultaneously increase inductor efficiency and reduce the overall power loss in cryogenic power conversion systems.

</details>

Abstract: Learning safe policies that generalize to unseen environments is difficult under bounded actuation, where an unsafe decision may become unrecoverable. Conventional control barrier function (CBF) filters enforce only instantaneous safety and ignore future recoverability. We propose SAFTER (Safety-Aware Filtering Through Explicit Recoverability), which embeds a differentiable, closed-form backup-CBF filter into Soft Actor-Critic training. SAFTER unifies current safety, finite-horizon backup safety, terminal recoverability, and input admissibility into a single differentiable certificate. Its CBF condition yields an exact minimum-intervention projection without iterative online optimization. In quadruped navigation, SAFTER reaches 90% training success with 30.6% fewer transitions than Vanilla SAC and 34.1-35.1% fewer than current-state CBF-filtered SAC baselines. Across unseen simulated environments, SAFTER improves success by up to 42.4 and 61.4 percentage points over the state-of-the-art methods CBF-RL and SEA-Nav, respectively, and achieves 73.3% and 76.7% success on two real-world navigation maps. Ablations identify accurate differentiation through the safety filter as a key contributor to robustness, in which replacing the projection Jacobian with the identity approximation reduces success rate in a High-Shift environment from 61.4% to 6.2%. These results show that recoverability-aware filtering serves as both a runtime safety mechanism and a learning mechanism for sample-efficient, robust policies.

Abstract: Motivated by ten years of Learning-Based Control Invited Sessions at the IEEE Conference on Decision and Control (CDC), this tutorial paper reviews key advances at the intersection of machine learning and control. We focus on methods that combine the flexibility of machine learning techniques with the structure and theoretical foundations of control to design autonomous systems that are capable, reliable, and safe. Abstract: We study worst-case finite-time energy dissipation in quadratic systems with proved global asymptotic stability (GAS). Estimating the maximum retained energy fraction to any fixed positive additive error below $1/2$ is NP-hard, even when the input includes a strict quartic Lyapunov function and proof of strictness, both constructed in polynomial time. NP denotes nondeterministic polynomial time. For a partially damped family, the unit-sphere minimum norm of the coupling map equals the structured coefficient distance to loss of GAS. Positive distances at most $1/2$ give two-sided inverse-square halving-time bounds with explicit coefficient factors. Polynomial-factor approximation of the least dissipated fraction, halving time or coefficient distance is NP-hard. Additive hardness persists throughout an explicit coefficient neighborhood and under direct damping with exponential stability, a strict quadratic Lyapunov function and a Hurwitz Jacobian. Unless $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{NP}$, where P denotes deterministic polynomial time, no polynomial-time algorithm attains these guarantees on all inputs.

</details>

Abstract: Mobile base stations (MBSs), including aerial and ground-based platforms, provide flexible wireless coverage through adaptive positioning. However, jointly optimizing their trajectories and digital beamforming is challenging because movement changes channel conditions and interference, while beamforming determines the resulting signal and interference powers. These coupled decisions also account for how current movements affect future positions and communication performance under mobility and safety constraints. We formulate this problem as a constrained Markov decision process that maximizes the time-averaged sum signal-to-interference-plus-noise ratio (SINR), subject to mobility, restricted-zone avoidance, and transmit-power constraints. To support long-term planning and reduce the extensive environment interactions required by model-free reinforcement learning, we develop a SafeDreamer-based world model framework that learns latent system dynamics from experience and optimizes a joint trajectory and beamforming policy through imagined trajectories. Feasibility-preserving action mappings and safety cost signals incorporate operational constraints into policy learning. Simulation results demonstrate that the proposed framework improves the time-averaged sum SINR by an average of 7\% and 9\% compared with proximal policy and deep Q-network baselines, respectively, while achieving higher sample efficiency and more reliable constraint satisfaction.

Abstract: Model predictive control is a high-performance control method, but it incurs comparatively high online computational costs across many control tasks and is challenging to realize in low-level hardware settings. To address this, we present a framework for generating neural network controllers that provably inherit the safety and stability guarantees of a robust model predictive policy. In contrast to approaches based on post-verification or additional online safety filters, our method provides constructive, a priori design conditions that ensure preservation of recursive feasibility and stability before deployment. We treat the approximation error as a bounded input disturbance, which is addressed in the MPC design. This allows the neural network controller to directly inherit robustness from the underlying model predictive controller, which enables the transfer of guarantees to the approximation. The design process is an offline, constructive synthesis pipeline and can be implemented once Lipschitz bounds are specified. We further demonstrate in simulation that the learned controller reproduces the closed-loop behavior of the original controller while reducing online computation.

Abstract: Mobile edge services can combine local computation with satellite and cellular access to deliver time-sensitive results in areas with intermittent terrestrial coverage. Meeting their deadlines and quality requirements calls for joint computation and transmission scheduling that accounts for task dependencies, changing link capacity, and shared resource budgets. However, optimizing resource allocations or tuning parameters within a fixed policy structure offers limited scope to correct failures rooted in the scheduling logic. We propose SatCraft, a framework in which a large language model (LLM) agent uses execution feedback to revise the code of joint scheduling policies. Each revision undergoes policy checks and complete-run evaluation to assess its validity, runtime, and effects on service completion. Because policy performance varies with access conditions, SatCraft retains valid alternatives in a policy library and selects one for each episode using initial access observations and the known resource configuration. Experiments using Starlink and cellular measurement data show that SatCraft improves cumulative completed-service value by 3.61%-16.89% over baselines, including recent state-of-the-art scheduling methods ADPRL, EdgeOPT, and TF-DDRL. It achieves the highest completed-service value in all four resource profiles and averages 25.02 ms per scheduling call. These results demonstrate how LLM-guided policy optimization can improve complete service delivery under the computing, communication, and quality constraints of satellite-cellular networks.

Abstract: Conservatism in distributed stability certification is often treated as a limitation of the certificate itself, motivating richer Lyapunov functions, tighter relaxations, or stronger optimization. But a more fundamental limitation may arise from the reporting interface: once information is compressed, physically different systems can become indistinguishable. If stable and unstable systems produce the same report, no sound method can separate them. This paper asks where this information limit lies and how much additional information is needed to move it.
  We model information as an information structure that partitions admissible systems into information fibers. The strongest sound certificate for a fiber is determined by its smallest stability margin, yielding a method-independent benchmark. This separates total conservatism into information-induced and method-induced parts and characterizes information order, task sufficiency, and the stability value of additional information. Three network-node studies show that network-spectrum, scalar/eigenvalue-only passivity, and independent-local reports respectively lose network--node alignment, local directional alignment, and cross-node relation. They show that critical missing information is often relational rather than dimensional.
  We then move from diagnosis to information design. An information-conservatism frontier gives the smallest irreducible conservatism achievable under a prescribed budget, while its inverse gives the minimum information required for a target conservatism. A witness-pair characterization turns this into a weighted covering problem. Besides, a local sensitivity law provides a computable approximation and identifies valuable unresolved quantities. The framework indicates whether conservatism should be reduced by improving the certificate or enriching the information.

</details>
